## **COURSE FILE** 

## **PC722: Fundamentals of Design - I (Drawing, Observation & Composition)** 

## _**Credit Structure:**_ **3-0-4-5** 

_**Program:**_ MDes (Communication Design) 

_**Year:**_ 1 

## _**Semester:**_ 1 

_**Duration:**_ 18 Days (Full Day – Lecture + Studio) _**Timing:**_ 9 AM – 6 PM 

_**Course Instructor:**_ Anirban Dutta Gupta + Visiting Faculty 

**Couse Placement:** Core course for MDes (CD) Semester 1. This course is composed of two parts – part A: Drawing and Observation and Part B: Composition. Part A and Part B is taken consecutively. This is a foundational course in design. 

**Course Format:** Module based. The course is taught as a combination of Lectures and Studio sessions. Lectures introduce concepts, which are then analysed and explored via studio sessions and assignments. Lecture and studio is integrated and in certain cases can overlap. Teaching is through hand-on learning and through learning-by-doing and via real-time demonstrations and guidance by faculty members. 

## **Course Objective and Content:** 

Design is an interdisciplinary activity – characterized by the constant preoccupation of corelating disciplines, dealing with and trying to understand the complex and interesting worlds of the physical, biological, imaginary, and human, all of which are multivariate in character. ‘The Fundamentals of Design 1’ course is an attempt to sensitize you to this complex, dynamic and multidimensional scenario – to expand the focus of your discipline, and place you within the larger context and discourse of design. The main objective of this 

Course Abstract: PC 722 FOD 1 for MDes CD 

1 

module is to acquaint students with the means and methods of approaching, investigating and solving problems creatively by providing them with appropriate design and conceptual skill sets – one such primary skill being that of ‘Drawing and Observation’ and ‘Composition’. 

Drawing and observation are fundamental to design because they are the primary tools for understanding and translating the world around us. They help designers move from abstract ideas to concrete, viable solutions. Observation is the bedrock of the design process. It's about more than just looking; it's about seeing, and it’s about a mindful, purposeful study of people, objects, and environments. By observing users in their natural habitats, designers can gain empathy and uncover unspoken needs, pain points, and behaviors that people may not even realize they have. This direct insight is crucial for creating designs that are truly user-centred. Observation also helps designers to gather data, identify opportunities by noticing what is and what is not working and find inspiration through observing nature, art, and daily life that can spark new ideas and creative connections between disparate concepts. 

Following on from observation, Drawing is the language of design. It's not about creating a perfect piece of art, but about a practical tool for thinking and communicating. Drawing gives shape to abstract ideas, allowing designers to quickly externalize and refine their thoughts. As a critical skill drawing helps in visual thinking and work through problems, explore multiple solutions, and develop ideas rapidly. It's far faster and more flexible than using digital tools in the early stages of a project. 

The act of drawing forces you to look at a subject more closely, paying attention to details like proportion, form, light, and texture. This trains your eye to see the world with a designer's perspective. By adding a layer of critical and reflexive thinking, drawing becomes a tool for communication – a rapid way to visually communicate the idea, something that is visual and tangible, which otherwise would be challenging to use words to communicate. 

Course Abstract: PC 722 FOD 1 for MDes CD 

2 

While Part A: Drawing and observation trains the eye to see and the hand to represent, Part B: Composition introduces the critical process of strategically arranging of elements to create a cohesive, clear, and impactful message. A good composition helps one control how a user's eye moves through a design, ensuring they see the most important information first. Without it, a design can feel chaotic, cluttered, and confusing, making it difficult for users to understand its purpose. Composition relies on several key cognitive principles (for example the Gestalt Theory) that work together to create an effective design. Composition looks at visual hierarchy which is the order in which a viewer's eye is drawn to elements. One can create a visual hierarchy by adjusting size, colour, contrast, and position to make some elements stand out more than others. Using the broad concepts if visual balance, contrast, rhythm and harmony and integrating with concepts of proximity and repetition the design can be perceived as a structured group. By grouping related items, one makes information easier to scan and understand. By using consistent elements like a specific colour palette, typography, or spacing, the design creates a sense of unity and reinforces a communication and identity, making the design feel cohesive and professional. By integrated negative space (or White Space) it gives the user's eye a place to rest, prevents the design from feeling cluttered, and can be used to emphasize a focal point by isolating it from other elements. 

For any design, whether digital interfaces or analog products, composition directly impacts user experience. It determines whether a user can quickly find what they're looking for and accomplish a task. A good composition increases comprehension with a well-composed design reducing cognitive load. The visual hierarchy guides them, making the information easy to process. It also improves usability by can creating intuitive layouts. This leads to higher conversion rates, lower bounce rates, and a more pleasant experience for the user. 

Beyond function, a good composition is simply more visually appealing. It creates a sense of harmony and professionalism that builds trust and makes the user want to engage with the design. 

Course Abstract: PC 722 FOD 1 for MDes CD 

3 

This module emphasizes the latitude and value of individual thinking and one’s ability to observe and see, which would enable you to apply yourself creatively in solving problems. Composition in design terms is defined as structured, thoughtful, compatible placement or arrangements of elements in space (2-dimension/3-dimension/4-dimension) and/or time. Composition allows us to incorporate varied elements, combining them into reasonable and working whole – an example that can be referred to here can be that of cooking – by combining varied elements in a particular manner and in particular proportions it produces a palatable preparation. Good composition conveys information, tells a story, draws attention to the important and useful bits, segregates data, brings in elements of aesthetics and leaves an indelible imprint in the mind’s eye. 

This is a critical foundational course in design. 

## **Course Outcomes:** 

## _**Knowledge:**_ 

1. Observation of objects, nature, indoor, outdoor 

2. Observation and imagination 

3. Hand, eye, perception 

4. Representation, Perspective & Proportion 

5. 1-point, 2-point & 3-point perspective 

6. Analytical and isometric drawings 

7. Human sketching and capturing motion 

8. Distilling to capture the essence 

9. Composition and its rules 

10. Principles of Composition: Balance, Contrast, Harmony, Rhythm, Repetition, Proximity, Negative Space, Figure-ground 

11. Gestalt Theory and its application 

12. Application of Gestalt Laws 

13. Nature of 2D and 3D spaces 

14. Composition in the real world 

Course Abstract: PC 722 FOD 1 for MDes CD 

4 

## _**Skill:**_ 

1. Drawing & Sketching skills 

2. Understanding tools – pencil, pen, paper, ink, brush 

3. Quality, precision and professionalism 

4. Visualisation and eye-trace training 

5. Judgement 

6. Communication 

7. Reflexive thinking 

**Assessment Method:** Continuous assessment of application of concepts through the duration of the course, along with assessment of group and individual assignments given. Assessment will not only cover the assignments, but professionalism, precision, pride, care and discipline displayed by the students.

This course prepares the students for professional application of these basic guidelines which will be applied in all design fields going forward.

## **COURSE FILE** 

## **PC732: Fundamentals of Design - II** 

## _**Credit Structure:**_ **3-0-4-5** 

_**Program:**_ MDes (Communication Design) 

_**Year:**_ 1 

_**Semester:**_ 1 

_**Duration:**_ 18 Days (Full Day – Lecture + Studio) 

_**Timing:**_ 9 AM – 6 PM 

_**Course Instructor:**_ Anirban Dutta Gupta + Visiting Faculty 

**Couse Placement:** Core course for MDes (CD) Semester 1. This course is composed of two parts – part A: Typography and Part B: Colour. Part A and Part B is taken consecutively. This is a foundational course in design. 

**Course Format:** Module based. The course is taught as a combination of Lectures and Studio sessions. Lectures introduce concepts, which are then analysed and explored via studio sessions and assignments. Lecture and studio is integrated and in certain cases can overlap. Teaching is through hand-on learning and through learning-by-doing and via real-time demonstrations and guidance by faculty members. 

## **Course Objective and Content:** 

This module deals with the advanced aspects of colour, composition, virtual 3D spaces, Typography as image, Illustration for information design, as means of explore, visualize and communicate complex ideas with high levels of data density. 

Course Abstract: PC 732 FOD 2 for MDes CD 

1 

This module would present an entry point to moving image and sound analysis, structure, methodology, concepts and experimentation for communicating specific ideas as well as to explore the possibilities and potential of each medium. Consideration of ‘mediums’ would focus on moving image scenarios that would include cinema, animation, multimedia and Sound Design and for 2D and virtual 3D environments. 

Students will also engage with ‘Design History’ as a means of understanding visual language that is applied for range of visualization, essential for practice of sketching, photography, film and animation. 

Typography is the art and technique of arranging type to make written language legible, readable, and visually appealing. It involves selecting typefaces (fonts), adjusting the spacing between letters (kerning), words (tracking), and lines (leading), and overall creating a visual style that complements the content and enhances the message being conveyed. 

Key elements of typography include Typeface which is the design of the letters with each shape communicating a distinctive emotion, which evolves into a font which are the variations within the family for example, Helvetica Bold or Times New Roman Italic. Each type and its categories are steeped in history as it is usage and the message it communicates. For example, the gothic Germanic type is associated with the fascist ideology, hence its usage today may lead to negative interpretation with the values associated with fascism. 

Beyond the selection of type, the second level is to manipulate the font to match to its usage. This includes understanding kerning - the space between two specific letters to improve legibility or visual balance, tracking - the space between all letters in a word or sentence, leading - the vertical space between lines of text, hierarchy - the arrangement of type elements to show their relative importance, often using different font sizes, weights, or styles to guide the reader, alignment – whether to position it left, right, center, or justified and contrast which is the differences in weight, size, or colour to create emphasis and improve readability. 

Course Abstract: PC 732 FOD 2 for MDes CD 

2 

Good typography can significantly affect how a message is perceived and can even evoke different emotional responses from the audience. It enhances readability and legibility ensuring that the text is easy to read. It helps in establishing a Visual Hierarchy by organizing information by signalling what’s most important. This overlaps with some of the concepts that were discussed in FOD 1 – Composition module. 

Typography also conveys tone and emotion, for example, Serif fonts (like Times New Roman) tend to be seen as formal, classic, and reliable. Sans-serif fonts (like Helvetica) are more modern, clean, and minimal. This leads into type as an element of brand design be creating a distinctive identity (Coca-Cola for example). It also helps in the aesthetic appeal with the right combination of font choices, spacing, and layout can make a design feel polished, cohesive, and visually striking, which grabs attention and makes an impact. 

In the digital domain, typography is essential for a smooth user experience. If the type is hard to read, poorly spaced, or mismatched, users will likely get frustrated and leave. Consistent and accessible typography helps users navigate the content effortlessly. 

Overall it guides the message by shaping how the message is understood and sets the mood for the audience. The wrong choice of typography could make a brand appear unprofessional, outdated, or inconsistent with its message. Typography, therefore, is a critical component of visual design because it goes beyond simply choosing a font. It's the art and technique of arranging type to make written language legible, readable, and appealing when displayed. This part of the module aims to sensitise the students to the variations of types and its uses. 

The objective of part B i.e. Basics of colour is to sensitise the students to the ‘idea’ of colour and understand that there is no ‘one’ colour. Colour is tainted by light, culture, our perception, biology, emotion, chemistry and physics. Within this maze, we will try and identify and understand some of the broad rules that will allow us to make sense of colour. The objective is to teach and familiarize students with different methods of determining 

Course Abstract: PC 732 FOD 2 for MDes CD 

3 

successful colour schemes that will enhance their designs, as well as the emotional effectiveness that those choices can have in the marketplace. As part of this course, we will understand colour theory and derivations of colour. It will also acquaint you to shades, tints and tones as well as colour harmony and colour interaction. This overall understanding of colour will be an essential stepping stone to take conscious decisions on application of colour. 

Colour is an integral component of design, playing a crucial role in shaping the visual and emotional experience of an audience. Colour is one of the most immediate and influential elements in design. The strategic use of colour can transform a design from a mere visual arrangement to a meaningful experience. The psychological effects of colour have long been studied in fields such as marketing, psychology, and design. Different colours evoke distinct emotional responses, which can influence perceptions and behaviour. For example, warm colours are commonly associated with energy, warmth, and urgency, while cool colours like often evoke calmness, trust, and professionalism. Understanding these emotional associations allows designers to select colours that align with the desired mood and tone of the design. It therefore, plays a fundamental role in establishing and reinforcing brand identity. The selection of a particular colour palette is often a deliberate decision aimed at differentiating a brand from its competitors while conveying specific values and messages. Consistent use of colour across various media platforms enhances brand recognition and fosters a sense of familiarity and trust with the audience. 

Beyond its aesthetic and emotional impact, colour is also critical to the functionality and accessibility of a design. In digital design, for example, the contrast between text and background colours directly affects readability. A lack of sufficient contrast can make content difficult to read, leading to a poor user experience. Therefore, colour choices must be made with consideration of legibility and ease of navigation. 

Course Abstract: PC 732 FOD 2 for MDes CD 

4 

Colours also carry specific cultural meanings that vary across different regions and societies. The students are encouraged to link the use and significance of colour to the course in Culture and Communications. For instance, in Western cultures, white is often associated with purity and peace, whereas in some Eastern cultures, it symbolizes mourning and death. Designers must be aware of these cultural connotations to avoid miscommunication or unintended negative associations when designing for global audiences. Moreover, colour symbolism can be employed strategically to reinforce the intended message of a design. 

Colour is fundamental to the creation of aesthetic cohesion and visual harmony within a design. A well-thought-out colour palette can unify disparate elements, creating a balanced and pleasing composition. The principles of colour theory, including complementary, analogous, and triadic colour schemes, guide designers in selecting harmonious combinations that are visually engaging and balanced. Colour adds another dimension to the principles of composition that was explored in FOD 1.

10. Type and design

## **COURSE FILE** 

## **PC749: Image, Text & Sound** 

## _**Credit Structure:**_ **3-0-4-5** 

_**Program:**_ MDes (Communication Design) 

_**Year:**_ 1 

_**Semester:**_ 1 

_**Duration:**_ 18 Days (Full Day – Lecture + Studio) _**Timing:**_ 9 AM – 6 PM 

_**Course Instructor:**_ Anirban Dutta Gupta 

**Couse Placement:** Core course for MDes (CD) Semester 1. This is a foundational course in design and is aimed to integrate the inputs in the previous courses into a ‘multimedia’ matrix and orient the students to the application oriented Semester 2 that follows. 

**Course Format:** Module based. The course is taught as a combination of Lectures and Studio sessions. Lectures introduce concepts, which are then analysed and explored via studio sessions and assignments. Lecture and studio is integrated and in certain cases can overlap. Teaching is through hand-on learning and through learning-by-doing and via real-time demonstrations and guidance by faculty members. Students will be expected to fieldwork. 

## **Course Objective and Content:** 

This course aims to introduce to elements of multi-media in Communication Design. It will encourage students to explore how words, images and sounds are put together for a range of communication events and forms. Students would be expected to observe, research, document and compose presentations deploying multi-media. They will be encouraged to read and explore ideas from design aesthetics and semiotics to understand compositional 

Course Abstract: PC 749 Image, Text and Sound for MDes CD 

1 

styles and aesthetic choices in traditional as well as contemporary forms of communication media in which visual, verbal and aural elements are combined to make effective communication events. Students would be able to appreciate diverse technologies of communication in rural or urban, India or in local or global media platforms and explore how image, text and sounds are combined to generate a context-specific meaning in specific contexts. This could be exemplified in case of advertisements, mounting of event such as theatrical, exhibition, and installation or ritual performances. By the end of the semester students are expected to acquire the skills and the theoretical insights that are needed to understand the relationships between composition and context in multimedia formats. 

In communication design, the integration of image, text, and sound is essential to creating effective, engaging, and immersive user experiences. This course investigates the individual and collective roles of these three elements in crafting messages that resonate emotionally, intellectually, and sensorially with the audience. The synergy between these elements results in a more comprehensive and powerful communication design, one that goes beyond traditional, single-sensory approaches to deliver a richer, multisensory experience to effectively convey messages, elicit responses, and shape user experiences. The fusion of image, text, and sound plays a pivotal role in determining the effectiveness of a design. While each element serves distinct functions, their interaction creates a more nuanced and dynamic experience that is capable of engaging multiple senses simultaneously. 

Images are integral to communication design because they convey meaning and emotion with immediacy. The power of visual imagery lies in its ability to transcend linguistic barriers and communicate universal concepts. Photographs, illustrations, icons, and graphics all contribute to the design by evoking emotional responses, by simplifying complex information and by providing context and meaning. Images establish the setting, provide visual cues, and help to reinforce or even redefine the message conveyed by accompanying text. 

Text remains the primary medium for delivering detailed information, articulating complex ideas, and guiding audience understanding. While images and sound evoke emotions and 

Course Abstract: PC 749 Image, Text and Sound for MDes CD 

2 

set tone, text provides the clarity and specificity necessary for the effective transmission of key messages. The strategic use of typography (comprehensively studied and deployed in Fundamentals of Design 2) and written language offers several advantages in communication design through clearly conveying information, by setting tone and voice, by enhancing accessibility through more legible text design, and finally, by providing structure and navigation through an organizational framework within a design. 

Sound is an often-overlooked yet powerful element of communication design, particularly in digital and multimedia contexts. It adds an additional layer of sensory engagement that heightens emotional connection and enhances the user experience. The use of sound effects, music, and spoken word in design helps to enhance emotional engagement, establish atmosphere and immersion, especially seen in films and in multimedia applications, games, and websites and in the digital realm support user interaction through haptic feedbacks. 

When combined, image, text, and sound can create a powerful, unified communication design platform. The interaction of these elements enables designers to convey messages that are not only clear and informative but also emotionally compelling and immersive. The integration of these media offers several advantages through multisensory engagement, memory retention and recall, enhanced emotional impact and an improved user experience.

3. Sound and emotion

5. Sound, Image, Text and culture 

6. Impact of sound, image and text in communication design

**Course Outcome:** The strategic use of image, text, and sound in communication design is fundamental to creating designs that are effective, engaging, and memorable. These elements, when used independently or in combination, enhance emotional engagement, clarify complex ideas, establish brand identities, and create immersive experiences. The synergy between image, text, and sound forms the backbone of modern communication design, offering a more dynamic and multisensory approach to conveying messages. By understanding the distinct and collective roles of these elements, designers can craft powerful, user-centered designs that resonate deeply with diverse audiences. This course is the pre-cursor course to more complex and challenging courses like Fundamentals of Filmmaking (Semester 2), Interaction Design (Semester 2) and Animation and Motion Graphics (Semester 3)

## **Masters of Design in Intelligent User Experience Design – Mdes (IUxD)** 

## **Semester -1** 

## **MI601:** 

## **Orientation to Intelligent User-Experience Design** 

## **Credit structure: 2-0-2-3** 

## **Content:** 

This course offers an insightful introduction to the Master of Design in Intelligent User Experience Design-IUxD. It delves into the rich history of digital design, tracing its evolution and highlighting pivotal practices that have shaped the field. Special emphasis is placed on the current landscape of the design industry in India & abroad, providing students with a comprehensive understanding of potential professional avenues. Through this course, students will gain a deeper appreciation for the objectives of the M. Des- IUxD and explore the diverse contexts in which design thrives today. 

## **Course Structure:  Module submission: 60%** 

**End Term Jury: 40%** 

_____________________________________________________________________________ 

## **MI602:** 

## **Fundamentals of Design for IUxD** 

## **Credit structure: 3-0-4-5** 

## **Content:** 

The module aims to equip IUxD students with the tools and techniques necessary to approach and solve design challenges in their field. This module emphasizes student's ability to observe and analyse, fostering creative problem-solving skills. Key areas covered include observation through drawing and understanding of composition. By closely observing users in their environments, students will gain insights into real-world behaviours, pain points, and motivations. Through the 

tool of sketching, students will learn to observe and analyse visual information, translating them into representations and identify patterns, synthesize information, and communicate findings. The compositional aspect extends observational skills and provides input for deliberate arrangement and organization of visual elements within a user interface. Students will learn about visual hierarchy to guide user attention and improve information consumption, usability, and efficiency. The module departs from traditional delivery to concentrate on the digital environment, introducing students to digital delivery technologies. 

## **Course Structure:   Module submission: 60% End Term Jury: 40%** 

______________________________________________________________________________ 

## **MI603** 

## **Elements of Design for IUxD** 

## **Credit structure: 2-0-4-4** 

## **Content:** 

Building upon the foundational understanding of design principles from the previous module, this module delves deeper into key concepts like colours, typography, and 3D space. Colour is explored as a powerful tool that goes beyond aesthetics, influencing user experience, guiding attention, and conveying meaning. In the context of IUxD specialization, colour plays a crucial role in creating visual hierarchies and highlighting essential elements, such as navigation cues or key information. Typography is recognized as an integral part of all fields of design including IUxD, influencing readability, accessibility, and overall usability. Students will learn to select appropriate typefaces by carefully considering various factors, given the vast number of available options. The concepts of space, form, and structure extend to understanding 3-Dimensional space, preparing students for work in wearable and phygital domains. A grasp of 3D space is essential for creating immersive experiences like VR, MR, and AR, as it provides the foundation for understanding how users interact with and navigate 3D spaces, objects, and interfaces. This knowledge also extends to 3D User Interfaces (3D UIs), enabling designers to go beyond the limitations of flat surfaces. Similar to the previous module, learning will be facilitated through interaction with digital technologies and platforms. While various platforms and templates offer easy access to compositions, colours palettes, and typography choices, it is crucial for students to understand the decision-making process behind these visual choices. 

## **Course Structure:   Module submission: 60% End Term Jury: 40%** 

_____________________________________________________________________________ 

## **MI604:** 

## **Intelligent Technology: An-introduction** 

## **Credit structure: 2-0-4-4** 

## **Content:** 

This course introduces students to the fundamentals of intelligent technologies, focusing on electronics, hardware, and sensor-based interactions. It covers the basics of Human-Computer Interaction (HCI) and its role in designing seamless user experiences. Through hands-on exploration with Arduino, Raspberry Pi, and visual programming tools like p5.js and Scratch, students will develop interactive prototypes that solve real-world problems. Emphasizing design thinking, sketching, and diagramming, the course encourages innovative idea generation. By the end, students will have the skills to create functional prototypes, bridging the gap between technology and user experience. 

## **Course Structure:   Module submission: 60% End Term Jury: 40%** 

______________________________________________________________________________ 

## **MI605:** 

## **Basics of UI, UX & IxD** 

## **Credit structure: 2-0-4-4** 

## **Content:** 

This course provides a comprehensive introduction to User Experience (UX) and Interaction Design (IxD), focusing on the key stages of the UX design process. Students will learn UX methodologies to understand user needs, followed by paper and digital prototyping techniques for creating intuitive interfaces. The course covers essential UI design principles, including color 

theory, typography, and UI elements, along with usability testing methods to refine designs. Additionally, students will explore design processes, conceptual models, and non-verbal humanmachine interactions, gaining insights into how users interact beyond traditional interfaces. By the end, students will be equipped with the knowledge and skills to research, design, and evaluate user-centered digital. 

**Course Structure:   Module submission: 60% End Term Jury: 40%** 

END 

**Course Title** : Optimization 

**Course Code:** IE402 

**Credit Structure** (L-T-P-Cr): 3-0-2-4 

**Prerequisites** :  Fundamental understanding of Linear Algebra, Matrix Theory. 

## **Suggested Textbook/references** : 

Operations Research by HA Taha 8[th] /9[th] Edition 

An introduction to optimization by E. K. P. Chong and S. H. Zak (Wiley) 

## **Evaluation Scheme** 

- In Semester Exam (30%) 

- End Semester Exam (50%) 

- Project (20%) 

The marks of all the components would be duly considered for Grading. This is tentatively the final schedule of evaluation. Any minor change will be announced well in advance. 

## **Course Outcome:** 

After the completion of course: 

- The students will be able to understand the application and working knowledge of optimization in selected areas. 

- The students will develop an insight and understanding with respect to formulation of optimization problems and designing the optimal solutions with applications in various domain and engineering applications. 

- The students will get acquainted with applications of optimization problems for some practical system model and few relevant work being carried out.

## **Lecture Plan/Contents:** 

1. Introduction to optimization methods [4 HOURS] 

   - Introduction 

   - Formulation of an LPP 

   - Graphical Method to solve an LPP 

   - Nature of a solution 

2. Linear programming problem [10 HOURS] 

   - Algebraic method 

   - Simplex method 

   - Revised simplex method 

   - Duality 

   - Dual simplex method 

   - Sensitivity analysis 

3. Transportation Problem [6 HOURS] 

   - North-west corner Rule 

   - Row-minimum method 

   - Vogel’s Approximation 

   - u-v method 

4. Game Theory [6 HOURS] 

   - Introduction to Game Theory 

   - Game with pure and mixed strategies 

   - 2 × n and m × 2 Games 

   - LPP Formulation of m x n Games 

5. Numerical Solution of Convex Optimization [8 HOURS] 

   - Gradient Descent, Steepest Descent 

   - Newton’s Method 

   - Conjugate Direction and Conjugate Gradient Methods

## **Course Outcome** : 

CO1. Improve the system performance by selecting a suitable controller and/or a compensator for a specific application 

CO2. Find model of the dynamical systems, such as a robots and quadrotors, and analyse their stability. 

CO3. Study and implement Path Planning algorithms that describe the motion of a robot between two points and generate trajectories such that the robots have safe and optimal motion. 

CO4. Design state estimation techniques such as Kalman and Bayes filters.

**Title of Course:** Human Computer Interaction **Code and Credit Structure:** IE403, 3-0-2-4 **Course Placement:** ICT & Technical Elective (RAS minor) **Course level:** Under **g** raduate **Instructors:** Prof. P.S. Kalyan Sasidhar, Office: 2109, Faculty Block 2, Extn. 560 Email: kalyan_sasidhar@daiict.ac.in 

## **Aims and Objectives:** 

This course will dive into the fundamentals of how man machine interaction happens and the principles governing the interactions. We will also look at how HCI relates to other fields like user experience design, user interface design, human factors engineering, and psychology. We will learn the three different roles of a user in interface design namely the ‘processor’ view, the ‘predictor’ view, and the ‘participant’ view. The course will cover human capabilities, design principles and models, prototyping and evaluation techniques. Emphasis will be given on learning and developing prototypes of various software modules, products etc. 

## **Course outcomes:** 

At the end of the course, students will understand: 

1. The relationship between HCI, user experience design, human factors engineering, and psychology. 

2. The feedback cycle in user interaction, including gulfs of execution and evaluation. 

3. Various Interaction design principles and heuristics in user interface design. 

4. The emerging ideas in HCI research, such as context-sensitive computing, gesture-based interaction, and social computing.

## **Course Conduct and Grading Policy** 

1. Students are supposed to be present for all lectures and lab sessions. 

2. If the attendance falls below 70%, a student will receive an F grade. (An institute-wide detailed policy will be communicated separately and will supersede this, once that takes effect) 

3. **All lab assignments must be completed** , failing which zero marks will be awarded for the lab component. One makeup lab session will be scheduled at the end of the semester for students who miss a session due to any reason. For special medical cases, if the Dean, AP has approved their application, additional makeup sessions may be provided to the concerned students. 

4. If a case of plagiarism/copying is detected in the exams or lab submissions, then that student will **receive zero marks** in the **complete lab segment (30%)** or the respective **exam component** . The student will also be reported to the Dean AP for possible further actions.

## IE494, Big Data Processing (Autumn’2025) 

**Course Placement** : B.Tech. (ICT), Technical Elective, Semester – VII 

**Course format** : 3 Lecture hours and 2 Lab hours. 

**Course Instructor:** PM Jat, 2203, Faculty Block 2 

## **Course Content:** 

Data has taken a different shape in today’s time. Modern applications and devices produce data that is very huge, unstructured, and cannot be fitted into the fixed schema. Such data, referred to as "Big Data" does not fit well in conventional storage and processing systems. 

A whole new set of techniques and technologies are emerging to process such data. This course teaches various techniques that are used for storing and manipulating "Big Data". 

## **Labs and Assignments:** 

There would be a series of lab exercises aimed to practice concepts learned in theory. Students will also be required to either do a project or a term paper. 

## **Evaluation plan:** 

1. Two Mid Semester Exams: 30% 

2. Project/Term Paper: 20% 

3. Lab Evaluation: 20% 

4. End Semester evaluation: 30% 

## **Course Outcomes:** 

Upon completion of this course, students should be able to 

- Explain why traditional data processing techniques do not work on big data problems. 

- Work on some of the popular large data processing frameworks like Hadoop, spark, and some NoSQL databases. 

- Able to perform various operations like filtering, projecting, aggregation, and OLAP queries on large datasets. 

- Appreciate Storage, Indexing, and query execution techniques for big data processing

## **Speech Communication (BTech =>CT 437/ PhD/MTech => CT 540)** 

## **Course Title:   Speech Communication** 

Credit Structure: (L-T-P-Cr):  3-0-2-4 

Prerequisites (if any): Basic Knowledge of Signals and Systems is desirable but will also be revised in the initial stage of the course. 

Course Offered as **Technical Elective** : BTech (ICT+CS for Sem V and Sem VII), MTech (ICT, WCSP, Sem III), MTech (ML as elective), MTech (VLSI => audit course) . MSc(DS, Sem III),  Open to PhD 

## A) **Grading Policy** 

Class Attendance = 15 % _1[st]_ In-Sem= 15 % 2[nd] In-Sem- 15 % Course Project =15 % Lab= 10 % Endsem =30 % 

- B) **Textbook:** I will be following a world-class textbook in the entire course 

**Textbook:** Thomas F. Quatieri, “Discrete-Time Speech Signal Processing,” Pearson Education, 2004. 

## C) **Exam Schedule and Syllabus w.r.t  Course Coverage** 

- 1) Course Project -> Students are encouraged to do projects in diversified areas (not limited to) **Deep Learning/Machine Learning** Applications to Speech Problems (such as Voice Conversion, Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR), Voice Assistants, Assistive Speech Technologies, Language Identification, Speech Enhancement, Speech Synthesis) 

- 2) 1[st] In-Sem  -> Chapter 1 (Introduction to Speech Communication),and Chapter 2 (Basics Required for the Course), **Generative Adversarial Networks** (GANs) for Speech Applications 

- 3) 2[nd] In-Sem -> Chapter 3 (Physiology of Speech Production), Chapter 4 (Acoustics of Speech Production) 

- 4) EndSem (Entire Syllabus) = More emphasis on => Chapter 5 Linear Prediction (LP) of Speech, Chapter 6 Cepstrum Analysis and MFCC 

## **Note:** 

- Fundamental knowledge of first six chapters is important perform well in Endsem, 

- Chapter numbers above refer to the Quatieri’s textbook mentioned above. 

**Course objective** :   To study fundamentals of speech production (physiological, acoustical and aeroacoustic perspective), speech analysis methods- STFT, spectrograms, LP analysis, and cepstrum analysis. A case study on Automatic Speech and Speaker Recognition.

|LAB 9|Estimation Fundamental Frequency (Fo) using Autocorrelation, LP Residual, Hilbert Envelope,<br>etc.|
|LAB 10|Case Study1: Convolutional Neural Networks (**CNN**) for Speech Applications|
|LAB 11|Case Study 2: Web-Scale Supervised Pre-training for Speech Recognition (**Whisper**)|
|LAB 12|Case Study 3: Generative Adversarial Networks (**GANs**) for Speech Enhancement, Voice<br>Conversion, etc.|

Course Instructor for Speech Communication (CT 437/CT 540), Associate Editor, IEEE Signal Processing Magazine 2021-2023 ISCA Distinguished Lecturer 2020-2022. APSIPA Distinguished Lecturer 2018-2019. Faculty Block-4, DA-IICT Gandhinagar Tel +91-79-30510-650 Email: hemant_patil@daiict.ac.in

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>
Speech Communication<br>Basic Signals and<br>Background readings<br>Systems<br>for course<br>Speech Production  Speech Analysis  Applications<br>Short-Time Fourier Analysis<br>Physiological factors       Speech Coding<br>Cepstral Analysis<br>Acoustics of Speech Production         Speech Recognition<br>Linear Prediction (LP) Analysis<br>Teager Energy Operator (TEO) and          Speaker Recognition<br>Applications<br>Language Identification<br>        Speech Synthesis<br>      Speech-to-Speech Translation<br>**----- End of picture text -----**<br>


**Fig. 1.** Activity tree for course on Speech Communication 

**Course Abstract:** - Speech communication is an interdisciplinary subject with different research areas ranging from electrical and communication engineering, linguistics, phonetics, psychology, computer science, etc. Speech communication is the transfer of information from one person to another or from person to machine via speech. This course equips students with different fundamental problems in speech science such as speech production process, speech perception, time-domain and frequency-domain processing, homomorphic speech processing and most popular linear prediction (LP) of speech and finally different diverse speech communication applications such as speech analysis, speech coding, speech synthesis, speech recognition and speaker recognition are discussed. The course is designed in such a way that relationships between different parts of speech communication can be understood easily. In addition to this, cohesive discussion on speech production and speech perception will help audience to understand speech communication process better. 

**Course Outline** : - Fig. 1 shows the activity tree for the course. The course will first review very briefly the basic concepts of DSP and random processes followed by detailed discussion on speech production process from the view point of physiological aspects to acoustic theory of speech production. The unique feature of the course will be a detailed discussion on aeroacoustic theory of speech production and development of Teager Energy Operator (TEO) and recently proposed variable length TEO (VTEO).

**IT462: Ex lorator Data Anal sis p y y** 

**Course placement:** This course is a technical elective for the BTech (semester VI, VIII) and MSc-DS (semester II) students. 

**Credit Structure:** 3-0-2-4 

**Course format:** It is **3 hours lecture** and **2 hours Laboratory** every week. 

**Course content:** This course provides students foundation for exploring data using the Python programming language. In particular, the numerical and graphical techniques for summarizing and displaying data. The course will cover the following contents: statistics and data, reading, cleaning, summarizing, and visualizing data using Python. 

**Textbook:** Exploratory Data Analysis by J W Tukey 

## **Reference books:** 

1. Visualizing Data by Cleveland 

2. Think Stats: Exploratory Data Analysis by Allen B. Downey, 2014 

3. Exploratory Data Analysis Using R by Ronald K. Pearson, CRC Press 

4. Graphical Data Analysis with R by Unwin Antony 

5. Graphical Exploratory Data Analysis by S. H. C. DuToit, A. G. W. Steyn, R. H. Stumpf 

**Assessment method:** Homework/Assignments, Online quizzes/tests in Moodle, Online discussion in Moodle, term examination and a Final Project. 

Final letter grades will be assessed on the following scale: 5% Attendance A [90% – 100%] 25% Quizzes/Assignments B [80% – 90%) **Grading Policy:** 30% Project/Tests/Viva C [70% – 80%) 40% Semester exam D [60% – 70%) F [0% – 60%) 

This grading policy is subject to change and the final grading policy will be updated once the class starts. 

**Course Outcomes:** The course’s primary aim is to introduce students to the graphical and modeling techniques for exploring data, emphasizing visualization, interpretation, and clear communication of findings. The course will use modern software tools for data manipulation and visualization. It will build a foundation for exploring data using Python. The intention is that the student develops an intuition for statistical data analysis. After completing the course, the student will have the ability to 

-  Import, manipulate, clean, visualize, and export data in Python. 

-  Wrangle data from its original format into a fit-for-purpose format. 

-  Conduct a systematic exploratory data analysis of different types of data. 

-  Identify, formulate and analyze complex engineering problems reaching substantiated conclusions using the first principles of mathematics. ( **P2** ) 

-  Use research methods, including design of experiments, analysis and interpretation of data, and synthesis of the information to provide valid conclusions. ( **P4** ) 

1 

-  Create, select, and apply appropriate techniques, resources, and IT tools, including prediction and modeling, to complex engineering activities with an understanding of the limitations. ( **P5** ) 

-  Recognize the need for and have the preparation and ability to engage in independent and life-long learning in the broadest context of technological change. ( **P12** )

_**Course Outline and Evaluation Policy SC471: Algorithmic Graph Theory during Autumn 2025-26 Course Instructor: V. Sunitha**_ 

_1. Title:_ Algorithmic Graph Theory 

_2. Credit Structure (L-T-P-Cr):_ 3 0 2 4 

## _3. Course Code_ _**:**_ SC471 

_4. Semester_ _**:**_ Sem V, Sem VII of B.Tech. (MnC) / Sem VII of B.Tech. (ICT, ICT-CS) / Sem I of M.Tech. (ICT) 

_5. Category_ _**:**_ MnC Elective / Science Elective / General Elective (Maths) 

## _6. Prerequisite:_ **Exposure to logical reasoning and proof techniques, ability to write neat computer programs, willingness to actively participate in classroom discussions.** 

_7. Objective/Motivation_ _**:**_ Graphs have a wide range of applications across various fields. They have proven to be an invaluable tool in establishing results in numerous areas of mathematics and, by extension, in engineering. Many graph-theoretic proofs are constructive, leading directly to the development of algorithms for solving diverse problems and puzzles. An engineer with a solid understanding of graph theory and its algorithms is better equipped to address and solve a broad array of complex challenges efficiently. 

_8. Outcomes_ _**:**_ The student should be able to model and solve real-world problems algorithmically using graph-theoretic techniques, while also providing a proof of algorithm correctness. In cases where efficient algorithms are unavailable, the student should be capable of developing practical, effective alternatives. Furthermore, the student should experience a notable improvement in both analytical and programming skills. 

_9. Topics (not including definitions, examples and other trivialities):_ 

- **A collection of results with emphasis on the following fundamental theorems and algorithms on graphs:** 

|**algorithms on graphs:**|**algorithms on graphs:**|
|---|---|
|||
|**_Theorems_**|**_Algorithms_**|
|Handshaking lemmas, Havel-Hakimi<br>theorem, Erdos and Gallai theorem,<br>The matrix-tree theorem, Cayley-<br>Kirchoff theorem, Menger’s<br>theorem(s), Characterization of<br>Eulerian graphs, Dirac’s theorem,<br>Ore’s theorem, Chvatal’s theorem,|Graph traversal algorithm(s), Shortest path<br>algorithm(s), Warshall’s algorithm, Prufer<br>sequence and labeled tree construction,<br>Minimum spanning tree algorithm(s),<br>Fleury’s algorithm, Colouring algorithm(s),<br>Planarity testing algorithm(s), Finding<br>strong components,Rankingin|



|Hall’s matching condition, Konig-<br>Egervary theorem, Tutte’s theorem,<br>Brook’s theorem, Vizing’s theorem,<br>Shannon’s theorem, Robertson-<br>Seymour theorem, Kuratowski’s<br>theorem,Five colourtheorem|tournaments.<br>In case of NP-complete problems,<br>polynomial algorithms available for special<br>cases will be discussed.|
|---|---|



_10. References:_ Vast resources on graph theory are available today and one has an easy access to many of these either online or in our RC.  The following collection is a good starting point. 

- A. Gibbons, Algorithmic Graph Theory, Cambridge University Press. 

- S. Even, Graph Algorithms, Computer Science Press. 

- D.B. West, Introduction to Graph Theory (2[nd] edition), Prentice Hall of India. 

- J.A. Bondy and U.S.R. Murty, Graph Theory with Applications, MacMillan Press. 

- G. Agnarsson and R. Greenlaw, Graph Theory: Modelling, Applications and Algorithms, Pearson Prentice Hall. 

- R. Diestel, Graph Theory (3[rd] edition), Graduate Texts in Mathematics Series, Springer. 

- J. B-Jensen and G. Gutin, Digraphs: Theory, Algorithms and Applications, SpringerVerlag. 

_11. Lab sessions:_ Mostly unsupervised and will involve writing graph algorithms, analysis as well as proof of correctness of these algorithms, and optionally computer programs for the algorithms.  A short introduction will be given for each lab assignment and the students are expected to work independently on the assignment.  Access to Institute’s Computing facilities for assignments requiring the same shall be provided as per the lab time-table for the course. 

_12. Assessment:_ The assessment will consist of the following components, each contributing 25% to the final grade: 

1. Exam-1 

2. Exam-2 

3. Exam-3 

4. Assignments and Quizzes 

Plagiarism of any kind will result in an 'F' grade. 

- **Exam-1** will take place during the in-semester 1 examinations, as per the Autumn 202526 Academic Calendar, and will cover topics taught up until that point. 

- **Exam-2** will be held during the in-semester 2 examinations and will cover topics taught after Exam-1. 

- **Exam-3** will take place during the end-of-semester examinations, covering topics taught after Exam-2. 

If the number of students enrolled in the course permits, a project may be introduced as part of the evaluation. In that case, the weightage for all components will be adjusted equally. 

## _13. Placement, work load, illness, and other absence:_ **There shall be no re-exam or make-up exam.  Any assignment not submitted on time and all unattended quizzes shall be awarded zero marks.** 

## _14. Grading Policy:_ **Absolute grading as per the following (tentative) scheme**

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**Grade**|AA|AB|BB|BC|CC|CD|DD|DE|F|



## **Advanced Matrix Theory (SC 404)** 

**Course Placement** : It is an elective course for BTech MnC, ICT and CS program. 

## **Credit Structure:** 3-0-0-3 

**Prerequisite** : Linear Algebra (MC215/SC223), Real and Complex analysis (SC107/MC222) 

**Course Objective:** Notions of matrices are of fundamental importance in Mathematics as well as several other areas including Machine learning, Data Science, Computer vision, Networking and Optimization, Computer Science, Signal processing etc. This course aims to develop a strong foundation for matrix analysis, developing essential tools. The emphasis will be on analytic aspects as well as algebraic aspects of Matrix Theory. 

**Course content:** (The selection of topics is variable depending on time constraints.) 

1. Review: eigenvalues, eigenvectors, special types of matrices. 

2. Schur triangular form, spectral theorem, singular value decomposition, polar decomposition, generalized inverse, least squares solutions. 

3. Positive semidefinite and Positive definite matrices: Properties and characterizations 

4. Matrix norms, spectral radius formula, relationship between matrix norms and singular 

values. 

5. Perron–Frobenius theory: Matrices with positive and non-negative entries. 

## **Suggested Textbook/references:** 

[1] F. Zhang. Matrix theory. Basic results and techniques. Springer, 1999. 

[2] R.A. Horn and C.R. Johnson. Matrix analysis. Cambridge University Press, 2012. 

**Assessment method:** In-Sem 1 - 30%, In-Sem 2 - 30%, End-Sem - 40% 

## **Course Outcomes:** 

After completing the course, the students should be able to: 

- Understand basic concepts related to matrix theory [PO12] 

- Handle abstract ideas of mathematics and learn to write mathematical proofs [PO1, PO2] 

- Understand different types of representations of a matrix [PO4]. 

- Develop skills towards solving problems using properties and characterization of special matrices [PO3].

## **CT 505: Detection & Estimation Theory** 

DAU (Formerly DA-IICT), Autumn Semester 2025-2026 

**Instructor:** Dr. Abhishek Jindal 

**Course Credits (L-T-P-C):** 3-0-0-3 

**Course Level (UG/PG):** Senior UG & PG (For B.Tech 5th/7th and M.Tech 1st semesters) 

**Lecture Timings:** TBD 

## **Laboratory Timings:** TBD 

**Prerequisites:** For senior UG students - SC224: Probability & Statistics or MC216: Probability & Random processes, and SC223: Linear Algebra; For PG students: any course on probability at UG level 

## **Course Contents:** 

1. Maximum likelihood theory 

2. Minimum variance and best linear unbiased estimators 

3. Cramer-Rao Lower bound 

4. Bayesian, MMSE and MAP estimation 

5. Kalman filter 

6. Bayesian, Neyman-Pearson & Minimax detection 

7. Composite hypothesis testing, Generalized LRT 

8. Detection of random/deterministic signals in presence of noise 

**Assignments:** Although the course has no dedicated slot for the tutorial, analytical problems will be regularly given to be solved. The solution may or may not be required to be submitted. The problems shall help in preparing for the exams. 

## **Course References:** 

1. _Fundamentals of Statistical Signal Processing - Volume I: Estimation Theory_ , Steven M. Kay, publisher: Prentice Hall, 1993. 

2. _Fundamentals of Statistical Signal Processing - Volume II: Detection Theory_ , Steven M. Kay, publisher: Prentice Hall, 1993. 

3. _An Introduction to Signal Detection and Estimation (2nd Edition)_ , H. V. Poor, publisher: Springer-Verlag, 1994. 

4. _Statistical Inference for Engineers and Data Scientists_ , Moulin and Veeravalli, publisher: Cambridge University Press, 2019. 

5. _Statistical Inference (2nd Edition)_ , Casella and Berger, publisher: Duxbury Press, 2002. 

6. _Signal Detection and Estimation_ , Mourad Barkat, publisher: Arctech House, 2005.

Note that the 30% of absence includes all medical and co-curricular-activities related leaves. 

**Course Outcomes:** After completing the course, students will be able to:

## **Real Time Embedded Software (IE420 / EL434 / EL518)** 

**Course Placement:** B.Tech (EVD) / B.Tech (ICT) / M.Tech (ICT with Spl. in VLSI & ES) **Credits:** 3-0-2-4 

**Course format: 3 hours lecture** and **2 hours lab** every week. 

**Course content:** This course introduces the design and implementation of real-time embedded software systems with strict response-time requirements. It covers embedded hardware, real-time operating systems (RTOS), driver configuration, sensor interfacing, and real-time data streaming to cloudbased platforms. Through lectures and hands-on labs, students develop systems used in applications such as healthcare monitoring, smart infrastructure, IoT, and edge computing. 

## **Text Book** 

- [1] Computers as Components: Principles of Embedded Computing System Design ~ Wolf, Marilyn (Elsevier, 2012) 

- [2] Real ~ Time Systems Design and Analysis: Tools for the Required Materials: Grading: Practitioner. Laplante ~ Phillip A. & Seppo J. Ovaska (John Wiley and Sons, 2011) 

## **References** 

- [1] Q. Li and C. Yao, Real-Time Concepts for Embedded Systems, 1st ed. San Francisco, CA, USA: CMP Books, 2003. 

- [2] J. W. Valvano, Embedded Systems: Introduction to ARM Cortex-M Microcontrollers, Volume 1, 5th ed. Lexington, KY, USA: CreateSpace Independent Publishing, 2018. 

- [3] J. Yiu, The Definitive Guide to ARM Cortex-M0 and Cortex-M0+ Processors, 2nd ed. Oxford, UK: Newnes, 2015. 

- [4] E. White, Making Embedded Systems: Design Patterns for Great Software, 1st ed. Sebastopol, CA, USA: O’Reilly Media, 2011. 

- [5] A. Bahga and V. Madisetti, Internet of Things: A Hands-On-Approach, 1st ed. Universities Press, 2015. 

- [6] AWS, Microsoft Azure, and Google Cloud documentation 

- [7] Confluent, The Ultimate Data Streaming Guide, 2025. [Online]. Available: https://www.confluent.io/resources/ebook/streaming-use-case-guide/ 

## **Assessment Method**

|---|---|---|
|1.|Second In-Sem Exam|30%|
|2.|End-Sem Exam|30%|
|2.|Assignments & Labs|20%|
|4.|Project|20%|



1 

## **Course Outcomes (COs)** 

- CO1: Understand fundamental concepts and distinctions of real-time and embedded systems. 

- CO2: Analyze and configure hardware drivers and port real-time operating systems. 

- CO3: Apply software abstraction layers and implement real-time dashboards using GUI frameworks. 

- CO4: Integrate embedded systems with IoT/cloud platforms and stream sensor data in real-time. 

- CO5: Design and develop real-time data streaming system for practical domains.

## **MC214: Operating Systems (3-0-2-4) Autumn 2025-26** 

**Instructor:** Anish Mathuria **Lectures:** TBA **Practicals:** TBA 

**Prerequisites:** basic data structures, computer organization 

## **Objectives** 

This course provides an understanding of the fundamental structures and mechanisms used by an operating system. 

## **Topics to be covered** 

- Multi-programming: processes and threads, system calls, context switching, types of scheduling, scheduling metrics, scheduling policies 

- Concurrency and synchronization: race conditions, critical sections, mutual exclusion, Peterson’s algorithm, hardware support for locking, spin lock, ticket lock, blocking lock, semaphores, condition variables, implementation issues, deadlocks, lock-free data structures 

- Virtual memory: logical/physical address spaces, paging, protection, TLB, demand paging, copy-on-write, page replacement policies, implementation issues 

- File systems: hard-disk drives, device drivers, files and directories, hard links, symbolic links, file system implementation, i-nodes, file layout approaches, crash recovery, journaling, fast file system, solid-state drives, file systems for SSDs 

## **Learning outcomes** 

- At the end of the course, students will have an understanding of 

   - the main principles and techniques used to implement processes and threads as well as the different algorithms for scheduling; 

   - the problems related to concurrency, the basic synchronization mechanisms available as well as the techniques used to implement them; 

   - the data structures and components (both hardware and software) that are used to implement virtual memory; 

   - how data structures of a file system serve to map file names to blocks of a storage device.

Two in-semester tests and a final exam will take place at the officially scheduled exam time for the course.

- 40%     final exam 

- 20%     in-lab exercises 

- 10%     mini-projects 

## **Lab policy** 

- Lab attendance is mandatory. You will have to demonstrate your code for the inlab exercises to a TA as specified in the lab handout. If you fail to do so, you will lose points. There will be no makeup labs for any reason. 

- It is a course requirement that you do the programming assignments on Linux OS. If the code you submit does not compile on Linux or we are unable to run the executable, you will not receive credit.

## **EL 453 Nanoelectronics** 

**Course Placement:** Nanoelectronics is an Elective course for fourth year (7[th] semester) of B.Tech ICT program **,** M.Tech ICT 3[rd] Semester. 

## **Course format: It is 3 hours lectures** every week. 

**Prerequisite courses** :  Basic electronics ckt. (EL103) 

## **Course content:** 

The course on fundamentals of nanoelectronics is to aid the student understand the origin of a number of new exciting development in this field and empower them to take advantage of the opportunities ahead of them. The term nanoelectronics encompasses developments in electronics that entail device physics and structures below the 100 nm level. 

The first part of the course covers ITRS predictions about emerging research devices, the limitations of nano CMOS and various options to MOSFET optimization. The next part covers the definition of the term nanoelectronics and its relationship to the broader topic of nanotechnology. Following this, the topic begins with an introduction to the principles of quantum mechanics, the wave particle duality, wavefunctions and Schrödinger's equation, energy bands, electrical conduction and low-dimensional structures. Then, the course discusses on the emerging research devices for nanocircuits, their long-range potential, technological maturity and challenges. The objective is to pursue long term alternative solutions to technologies addressed in More-than-Moore ITRS entries. 

## **Books and literature** : 

1. Book title: Nanoelectronics: Physics, Technology and Applications, by Rutu Parekh and Rasika Dhavse, IOP publisher, UK. 

2. Research papers. 

## **Course Outcomes:** 

Students will gain knowledge about the future trends in the technology, the physics behind the devices, its implementation and use in circuits. The objective is to give them vision about the latest in technology and future predictions. This can open many avenues for their future career. 

In addition, they will work in group and conduct research in the area of emerging technology, perform simulation, analyze and discuss the results. If possible, publish a research paper.

## **Course Title:** Digital Programming 

**Course Code:** EL467 

**Course Credit: 3-0-2-4** 

**Prerequisite:** Basic understanding of digital logic design 

**Course Placement:** EL467 is an elective course for M.Tech. ICT (First Semester) and B.Tech. VII Sem (ICT/ICT-CS). 

**Course Content:** The course aims to impart digital logic concept realization using hardware description language (HDL) and its practical implementation on FPGA based hardware kits. The HDL will be discussed in detail with all its abstraction levels and modeling schemes. The focus of the course will be to develop practical realizable projects using HDL and FPGA. 

|**Sr. No.**|**Description**|
|---|---|
|**1.**|**Introduction to Digital Design, Concept of Hierarchical and Structured Design **|
||Digital meaning and essence, Basics of digital VLSI design, role of CAD tools in VLSI design process, Hierarchical<br>and design methodologies in VLSI systems, CAD algorithms for device, circuit, gate and system levels of<br>abstraction, Physical design process in VLSI design, Testing.|
|**2.**|**Introduction to HDL, Synthesis and FPGA **|
||Introduction to hardware description language, Basic language elements of Verilog, Behavioral modeling, Data flow<br>modeling, Structural modeling, Switch level modeling, Constructs and conventions in Verilog, Data types, Number<br>system, Operators.<br>Modeling and synthesis issues in Verilog, Familiarization with analog and mixed signal designing in Verilog.<br>Concept of CPLD, FPGA, Familiarization with Xilinx Spartan-3E FPGA development board, design constraints<br>using FPGA, implementing designs using FPGA.|
|**3.**|**Combinational Systems Realization Using HDLand FPGA **|
||Verilog description of gates, User-defined primitives, Instantiation, Design of arithmetic functions – adder,<br>subtractor, multiplier, divider, Implementation of multiplexer, demultiplexer, encoder and decoder, code converters,<br>Design of complex systems, Parameterized module, Realization of test benches, Timing and delay models.|
|**4.**|**Description and Design of Sequential Circuits**|
||Defining procedural blocks, Procedural flow control, Blocking and non-blocking assignments, Realization of latches,<br>flip-flops, register, counter, Implementation of synchronous and asynchronous designs.|



**5. Data Subsystems** 

Storage modules, Memory, Stack, Queue, Functional modules, Data paths, Control subsystems. 

**6. FSM and I/O Subsystem** 

Finite state machine, Mealy and Moore designs, System Task and functions, Operations related to I/O subsystems, Compiler directives, VeSPA (Very Small Processor Architecture) processor—developing behavioral and structural modeling using HDL and FPGA, Design of practical systems. 

## **Books:** 

1. Samir Palnitkar, Verilog HDL: A Guide to Digital Design and Synthesis, Prentice Hall. 

2. Stephen Brown and Zvonko Vrsaniec, Fundamentals of Digital Logic with Verilog Design, McGraw Hill. 

3. Zainalabedin Navabi, Verilog Digital System Design, McGraw Hill. 

4. Vivek Sagdeo, The Complete Verilog Book, Kluwer Academic Pub. 

5. Peter R. Wilson, Design Recipes for FPGAs, Elsevier. 

**Assessment Method:** Quizzes, 1 Project, In-Semester Examinations and a Final Examination will be conducted during the course. 

**Grading Policy: In-Sem 1:           20% In-Sem 2:           20% End-Sem:           30% Lab:                    10% Project, Quiz:    20%** 

## **Course Outcomes:** 

After successful completion of this course, 

1.  The students shall be able to define specifications and then design digital system conforming to those specifications. They shall be able to implement these designs efficiently using Verilog HDL. 

2. Extensive hands on training on FPGA based projects shall give confidence to the students to realize digital circuits on the hardware. This shall facilitate students to make state-of-the-art practical research projects and meet industrial demands.

## **Course Objective** 

Digital signal processing hardware is an important element in real time application that enhances the performance of the system. This course provides various VLSI architectures used in digital signal processing such as discrete orthogonal transformations, digital filtering etc. The course aims to provide advanced understanding of hardware designs of various Digital Signal Processing operations. 

**Course Outcome:** At the end of the course, students will be able to: 

- Understand the hardware and design concepts of DSP filtering techniques and transformations and their circuit implementations 

## **Syllabus** 

- Arithmetic Circuits 

- Building blocks for DSP Processors 

- Digital filter design 

- DFT architectures 

- Design of DSP Data ~ Path Blocks

## **Course Outcome:** 

**CO1:** Understand the principles of flexible electronics, including materials, processes and fabrication techniques for flexible electronic devices and circuits, with a focus on integration with traditional semiconductor systems. 

**CO2:** To develop flexible electronic devices such as thin-film transistors, printed circuits, sensors and other flexible devices, applying knowledge of process integration and reliability evaluation. 

**CO3:** Apply flexible electronics technologies to real-world applications in wearables, IoT, and beyond, while addressing challenges in performance, scalability, and system integration.

## **Flexible and Printed Electronics Lab** 

## **Lab Experiments** 

## **1. Analysis of surface properties in flexible substrates for printed electronics.** 

_**Objective** : To evaluate surface wettability for printed ink adhesion on flexible substrate using contact angle measurement._ 

_**Outcome:** Understand surface energy relevance in ink spreading and adhesion._ 

## **2. Development of conductive ink for flexible electronics and printing techniques.** 

_**Objective** : To prepare a basic carbon or silver-based ink and test its properties._ 

_**Outcome:** Learn principles of ink optimization for printing._ 

3 

## **3. Printing resistive and/or capacitive tracks using conductive ink on flexible substrate to evaluate their electrical behavior.** 

_**Objective** : To design and print resistive and/or capacitive structures on flexible substrates using conductive ink, and evaluate their electrical behavior._ 

_**Outcome:** Understand material resistive/capacitive behavior along with tracking geometry effects._ 

**4. Characterization of printed resistor using temperature-dependent resistance measurement to demonstrates TCR (temperature coefficient of resistance) using 2-wire method.** 

_**Objective** : To measure resistance of printed track as a function of temperature._ 

_**Outcome:** Demonstrates TCR (temperature coefficient of resistance) using 2-wire method._ 

## **5. Electro-physical analysis of moisture-induced conductance in printed tracks.** 

_**Objective** : To investigate how moisture exposure affects the resistance of printed conductive tracks._ 

_**Outcome:** Hands-on experience with resistive sensor characterization along with understanding of environmental effects on printed electronics._ 

## **6. Development of flexible electronics circuitry using conductive ink.** 

_**Objective** : To fabricate a simple flexible electronics circuit using conductive ink on a flexible substrate, and then assemble and test a basic electronic circuit._ 

_**Outcome:** Develop an understanding of how flexible electronics can be used in real-world applications._ 

## **7. Bending and reliability testing of printed conductive tracks.** 

_**Objective** : To study how repeated mechanical bending affects the electrical resistance of printed conductive tracks on flexible substrates._ 

_**Outcome:** Understanding mechanical reliability of flexible and printed electronics._ 

## **8. Fabrication and characterization of a flexible strain sensor using conductive ink.** 

_**Objective** : To fabricate a flexible strain sensor using conductive ink (e.g., carbon, graphene etc.) on a flexible substrate and study its response under applied strain._ 

_**Outcome:** Students will be able to measure and analyze the change in electrical properties with strain, demonstrating the sensor’s sensitivity and flexibility._

Specialization Core  – 66:33, Electives – 50:50. ## **IT486: Blockchains and Cryptocurrencies (3-0-0-3) Autumn 2025-26** 

## **Course Placement** 

Open to students in the final year of the B.Tech and M.Tech programs. 

**Pre-requisites:** You should know the basics of cryptography. 

**Instructor:** Anish Mathuria 

**Objectives:** This course explores the fundamentals of blockchains and cryptocurrencies. 

## **Course topics** 

Topics covered include blind signatures, anonymous electronic cash, decentralized cryptocurrencies, proof of work, Bitcoin blockchain, bitcoin transactions, Script language, micropayments, lotteries, cryptocurrency exchange, proof of solvency, atomic currency swap, mining, mining pools, chain splits, ECDSA/Schnorr signatures, signature aggregation, Pedersen commitment, confidential transactions, simplified payment verification, hierarchical key generation, unlinkable transactions, coin mixing, ring signatures, transaction malleability, smart contracts, and Ethereum. 

## **Course materials** 

We will use selected papers and references available online. The lecture slides will be made available in the Lecture folder as the course progresses. 

The following references are useful. 

- _Bitcoin and Cryptocurrency Technologies: A Comprehensive Introduction_ , Narayanan, et. al. Princeton, 2016.  ISBN: 978-0691171692. 

- _Programming Bitcoin_ , Jimmy Song, O’Reilly, 2019. ISBN: 978-93-5213-808-1. 

## **Evaluation (tentative)** 

- 20%    pop quizzes (in-lecture) 

- 40%    two in-sem exams 

- 20%    assignments (written + programming) 

- 20%    term paper 

## **Course Outcomes** 

Upon completion of the course students should be able: 

- to understand properties of blockchains and their applications 

- to understand the fundamental principles and algorithms underlying bitcoin 

- to understand the limitations of cryptocurrencies such as bitcoin

## **IT531 Advanced Computer Networks** 

Instructor: Dr. Abhishek Gupta Office: 3208, Faculty Block 3 Email: abhishek_gupta@dau.ac.in 

**Course Placement** : Advanced Computer Networks is an elective course offered to fourth year students of the B.Tech program and to M.Tech students. 

**Course format** : It is 3 hours’ lecture every week. 

**Prerequisites** : This course assumes knowledge of the first level B.Tech course in Computer Networks. Python and Matlab programming skills (optional) may be useful for the course project. 

**Course content** : Major topics in this course include: Internet protocols for congestion control, approaches to achieve reliability, scalability. Network measurement, P2P networks, Wireless networks, Content delivery networks. Software Defined Networking (SDN) architecture, Network Functions Virtualization (NFV). 

This course will emphasize the concepts and issues underlying the design and implementation of the Internet and other kinds of modern wireless networks. We will also spend time learning to quantitatively analyse the performance of network protocols. A combination of reference books and a set of research papers will be used to cover the fundamentals, seminal findings and new directions in networking research. This course will introduce students to the essential tools and concepts such as: 

- Understanding a computer networks’ basic structure requires concepts. 

- Visualize the connected structure where networks are not only limited to computers but span to billions of devices. 

- The course aims to develop a working foundation that would allow for systematic analysis of modern networks. 

- In addition, it also aims at exposing students to some modern challenges and selected methods that are used extensively in the research literature. 

- Understanding the technology of the Internet and learning how to improve its properties is a fundamental objective of this course that will have an enormously beneficial impact on all facets of the economy and society at large. 

**Course Goals** : The goals for this course are (1) To study main concepts of computer network architecture, protocols and systems; (2) To become acquainted with computer networking research topics; (3) To gain practice in reading research papers and discussing the research of others, and (4) To be introduced and gain experience with network programming, analysis of traffic measurements, traffic control algorithms, and peer-to-peer software. 

**Course Outcomes** : At the end of the course, the students should be able to analyse a communication system by separating out the different functions provided by the network; understand that there are fundamental limits to any network; understand the general 

1 

principles behind multiplexing, addressing, routing, reliable transmission and other protocols as well as specific examples of each; be able to compare various network architectures and how they solve similar problems with different efficiency; have an informed view of both the internal workings of the Internet and of a number of common Internet applications and protocols. After completing the course, the students would be able to apply the knowledge of computer networks to model complex problems in network engineering (P1,P2,P3,P12), carry out model based investigations to derive insight into the problem at hand (P4), communicate effectively through vivas, course presentation and report writing (P10). 

**Evaluation and Assessment** : There will be assignments, critical review of a research paper and a course project. The final grade will be based on the assignments (40%), critical review of a research paper (10%) and the final project (50%). 

The final project is broken down into following components: 

- 10% for Intermediate report (due in Mid-September) 

- 10% for Presentation 

- 30% for Final report (To be submitted in IEEE conference paper template, should be of minimum 6-pages, must be written using LaTeX and not MS-Word). 

The course project aims to gain an understanding of how networking may evolve in the future to provide ubiquitous support for quality-of-service (QoS) in heterogeneous environments. The students will identify and critically review one paper selected from either of the following journals/conferences (not limited to): 

- **IEEE/ACM Transactions on Networking** 

- **IEEE Transactions on Network Science and Engineering** 

- **IEEE Transactions on Network and Service Management** 

- **IEEE Transactions on Green Communications and Networking** 

- **IEEE Transactions on Wireless Communications** 

- **IEEE Wireless Communications and Networking Conference (WCNC)** 

- **IEEE Conference on Network Softwarization (NetSoft)** 

- **IEEE Conference on Local Computer Networks (LCN)** 

- **IEEE Conference on Computer Communications (InfoCom)** 

This will help students to get acquainted with some advanced research topics in computer networking, that will enable them to conduct state-of the-art research in various domains of networking. 

In the final project, the students will suggest ways to address issues that they found in the identified paper during the critical review stage. Through simulations, a proposed solution is expected to address that issue. For the final project, the students are allowed to work in a group of **maximum** 4 students. For final project submission, a single file (pdf only) should be submitted electronically to the instructor’s e-mail address and a printed hardcopy (black and white) must also be submitted. Please make sure that all the pages (and especially the figures) can be viewed properly in acrobat reader as well in the printed version. If the 

2 

project involves simulations, data analysis etc., all the necessary files should be stored (backed-up) by the group for future reference, but these are not required to be submitted. 

A single textbook is insufficient for this course. Some of the relevant resources that will be referred to are: 

Text books: 

- Computer Networks. Tanenbaum, A. S., & Wetherall, D. J. 6th ed., Pearson, 2022. 

- Computer Networking: A Top-Down Approach. Kurose, James F., and Keith W. Ross. 8th ed., Pearson, 2021. 

Reference books: 

- Computer Networks: A Systems Approach. Peterson, L. L., & Davie, B. S. 6th ed., Cambridge, MA: Morgan Kaufmann, 2022. 

- Stallings, W. (2017). Data and Computer Communications (10th ed.). Pearson Education India. 

- Data Communication and Networking, D P Nagpal, S.Chand Publishing, 2011. 

- Hassan, Mahboob and Jain, Raj, "High Performance TCP/IP Networking," Prentice Hall India. 

- Murthy, Manoj, “Ad Hoc Wireless Networks,” Pearson Education. 

## **Additional Books for extra reading:**
